\tilde{s}_{2}(\kappa)=\frac{12\kappa^{2}+1}{16}\left[\psi\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)-\ln\kappa\right]-\frac{1}{32}\,.

(4.112)

The remainder
r_{2}
, from expanding
S_{N}^{(0)}(E(\hbar))
with the energy correction
E=\hbar\kappa+\hbar^{2}(-\frac{3}{4}\kappa^{2}-\frac{1}{16})+\cdots
and extracting the finite part of
\hbar^{2}\,S_{N}^{(2)}
, contains the same digamma combination with the opposite sign. Adding
\tilde{s}_{2}+r_{2}
, the
\psi-\ln\kappa
terms cancel algebraically and the full
\hbar^{2}
coefficient is simply a polynomial:

S_{N}=\frac{2}{3}+\hbar\ln\left(\frac{\Gamma(\kappa+\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)-\hbar\kappa\ln\frac{8}{\hbar}+\hbar^{2}\left[\frac{17}{8}\kappa^{2}+\frac{19}{96}\right]+\hbar^{3}\left[\frac{125}{32}\kappa^{3}+\frac{153}{128}\kappa\right]+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{4})\,.

(4.113)

At
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})
the tower involves both digamma and trigamma functions, but after including the energy correction from
E_{P}\neq\hbar\kappa
, all special functions cancel and the full
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})
coefficient is again a pure polynomial in
\kappa
(see Appendix
C
for the derivation).

4.8
Energy spectrum

Finally, we are ready to solve the quantization condition
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}
, for the odd modes as in Eq. (
4.78
), or
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=-i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}
for the even modes, as in Eq. (
4.79
). From Eq. (
4.109
) we see that
S_{N}=\frac{2}{3}
at
\hbar=0
, so that
\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}=\exp(-\frac{2}{3\hbar})
. This is the WKB tunneling factor which is exponentially small. So let us define

\lambda=e^{-S_{I}/\hbar}=\exp\left(-\frac{2}{3\hbar}\right)

(4.114)

where
S_{I}=\frac{2}{3}
is the 1-instanton action in our normalization, and work order-by-order in
\lambda
.

To order
\lambda^{0}
we set
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}=0
. Then the quantization condition reduces to
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}+1=0
. Note that the
\pm
plays no role, so there is no splitting between even and odd modes. That is, in the absence of tunneling the even and odd states are exactly degenerate. This is the first non-trivial result about the double-well
spectrum.

Perturbative spectrum

For the perturbative spectrum we need to solve
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=-1
where
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=\exp(-\frac{2}{\hbar}S_{P})
with
S_{P}
in Eq. (
4.108
). The leading terms at small
\hbar
are

{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=\exp\left[-\frac{2\pi i}{\hbar}\left(E+\frac{3}{4}E^{2}+\frac{35}{16}E^{3}+\cdots\right)\right]\,.

(4.115)

To solve
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=-1=\exp[2\pi i(N+\frac{1}{2})]
we must have
E\sim\hbar
. The leading order result is then
E=\hbar\kappa
with
\kappa=N+\frac{1}{2}
which is, not surprisingly, exactly the spectrum of the SHO associated with either minimum. To get higher order corrections in
\hbar
to the perturbative spectrum, we expand systematically as

E=\hbar\kappa+\hbar^{2}e_{2}+\hbar^{3}e_{3}+\cdots\,.

(4.116)

So we want

\exp\left[\frac{2\pi i}{\hbar}\left(-\hbar\kappa-\hbar^{2}e_{2}+\cdots-\frac{3}{4}(\hbar\kappa+\hbar^{2}e_{2}+\cdots)^{2}+\cdots\right)-2\pi i\frac{\hbar}{16}\left(1+\frac{25}{4}\hbar\kappa+\cdots\right)\right]=-1

(4.117)

which means

-\hbar e_{2}+\cdots-\frac{3}{4\hbar}(\hbar\kappa+\hbar^{2}e_{2}+\cdots)^{2}+\cdots-\frac{\hbar}{16}\left(1+\frac{25}{4}\hbar\kappa+\cdots\right)=0\,.

(4.118)

Solving order by order leads to

E_{P}=\hbar\kappa-\hbar^{2}\frac{1+12\kappa^{2}}{16}-\hbar^{3}\frac{19\kappa+68\kappa^{3}}{64}-\hbar^{4}\frac{131+3672\kappa^{2}+6000\kappa^{4}}{2048}+\cdots\,.

(4.119)

This is in perfect agreement with the same expansion computed using Rayleigh-Schrödinger perturbation theory or the Bender-Wu approach, as discussed in Appendix
A
.

1-instanton sector

Next, we want to include non-perturbative corrections which depend on the tunneling factor. This is done by expanding perturbatively in
\lambda=\exp(-S_{I}/\hbar)
. To do so we write
E=E_{P}+\lambda E_{1}+\lambda^{2}E_{2}+\cdots
and solve
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)=\pm i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E)}
order by order in
\lambda
. To order
\lambda^{0}

{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E)=0
and
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)=0
giving
E=E_{P}
.

To first order in
\lambda
we can Taylor expand
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}
around
E=E_{P}
so that

\displaystyle 1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)

\displaystyle=\lambda{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})E_{1}+\cdots=\lambda E_{1}\frac{2}{\hbar}S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})+\cdots

(4.120)

\displaystyle=\lambda E_{1}\frac{2\pi i}{\hbar}\left[1+\frac{3\hbar\kappa}{2}+\frac{1}{64}\hbar^{2}\left(348\kappa^{2}+19\right)+\frac{3}{128}\hbar^{3}\kappa\left(1052\kappa^{2}+191\right)+O\left(\hbar^{4}\right)\right]\,.

For
\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}
, since it starts at order
\lambda
, we can evaluate it at
E=E_{P}
. Using Eq. (
4.111
) this gives

\pm i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}=\pm ie^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}S_{N}}=\pm\frac{i\lambda\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{E_{P}}{\hbar}+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{E_{P}/\hbar}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\right)\,.

(4.121)

With these expansions,
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E_{P}+\lambda E_{1}^{\pm})=\pm i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E_{P})}
lets us solve for
E_{1}^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{i\hbar}{2S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma\!\left(\frac{E_{P}}{\hbar}+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{E_{P}/\hbar}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\right)

(4.122)

\displaystyle=\pm\left(\frac{\hbar}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-\frac{3\hbar^{2}\kappa}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}+\cdots\right)\frac{1}{\Gamma\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}-\hbar\tfrac{1+12\kappa^{2}}{16}+\cdots\right)}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{\kappa-\hbar\tfrac{1+12\kappa^{2}}{16}+\cdots}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\right)\,.

To go to next order in
\hbar
still at order
\lambda
, it is cleaner to keep
\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}
in exponential form. Writing
\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E_{P})}=e^{-S_{N}(E_{P})/\hbar}=\lambda\,e^{-(S_{N}-S_{I})/\hbar}
and equating with Eq. (
4.120
), the
\lambda
factors cancel to give

E_{1}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i\hbar}{2S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}(S_{N}-S_{I})}\,,

(4.123)

which is exact in
S_{N}
(no resummation applied). Relying on Eq. (
4.113
) to expand
S_{N}-S_{I}
, we have

E_{1}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{\hbar}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{\kappa}\Bigg[1-\left(\frac{3\kappa}{2}+\frac{17\kappa^{2}}{8}+\frac{19}{96}\right)\hbar\\
+\left(\frac{289}{128}\kappa^{4}-\frac{23}{32}\kappa^{3}-\frac{2125}{768}\kappa^{2}-\frac{115}{128}\kappa-\frac{5111}{18432}\right)\hbar^{2}+O(\hbar^{3})\Bigg]

(4.124)

where
-\tfrac{3}{2}\kappa
arises from expanding
i\hbar/(2S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P}))
and
-\tfrac{17}{8}\kappa^{2}-\tfrac{19}{96}
comes from Eq. (
4.113
); the
\hbar^{2}
polynomial is obtained from the same expansion using
S_{P}
to
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})
in Eq. (
4.108
) and
S_{N}
to
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})
in Eq. (
4.113
), together with the perturbative energy correction
E_{P}=\hbar\kappa-\hbar^{2}(\tfrac{3\kappa^{2}}{4}+\tfrac{1}{16})+\cdots
. Evaluating at
\kappa=\tfrac{1}{2}
gives the even/odd ground-state splitting

\Delta E_{0}=\lambda(E_{1}^{+}-E_{1}^{-})_{\kappa=\frac{1}{2}}=e^{-\frac{2}{3\hbar}}\sqrt{\frac{16\hbar}{\pi}}\left[1-\frac{71\hbar}{48}-\frac{6299}{4608}\hbar^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})\right]\,,

(4.125)

while for the first excited states we have

\Delta E_{1}=\lambda(E_{1}^{+}-E_{1}^{-})_{\kappa=\frac{3}{2}}=e^{-\frac{2}{3\hbar}}\frac{32}{\sqrt{\hbar\pi}}\left[1-\frac{347\hbar}{48}+\frac{5317}{4608}\hbar^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})\right]\,.

(4.126)

2-instanton sector

The 1-instanton result determines the splitting between even and odd states. To find the common energy shift at
O(\lambda^{2})
we return to the squared quantization condition, Eq. (
4.74
):

(1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P})^{2}+{\mathcal{V}}_{N}=0

(4.127)

and expand
E=E_{P}+\lambda E_{1}^{\pm}+\lambda^{2}E_{2}+\cdots
. After this shift,
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)=\lambda{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})E^{\pm}_{1}+O(\lambda^{2})
, and
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E_{P})=O(\lambda^{2})
. At
O(\lambda^{2})
we recover

({\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P}))^{2}\,(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}=-\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E_{P})}{\lambda^{2}}

(4.128)

which determines
(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}
and reproduces the 1-instanton result above.

At
O(\lambda^{3})
, expanding to the next order and dividing by
E_{1}^{\pm}
gives

2({\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime})^{2}E_{2}+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}=-\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}^{\prime}(E_{P})}{\lambda^{2}}

(4.129)

where primes denote
\partial_{E}
and all quantities are evaluated at
E=E_{P}
. Using Eq. (
4.128
) and solving for
E_{2}
gives:

E_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}^{\prime}}{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}-\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime\prime}}{{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}}\right]\,.

(4.130)

Since this depends on
(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}
,
E_{2}
is the
same for even and odd states
: the 2-instanton sector contributes a common energy shift, not a splitting. More generally, odd instanton sectors (
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{3},\ldots
) produce splittings while even instanton sectors (
\lambda^{2},\lambda^{4},\ldots
) produce common shifts.

To evaluate Eq. (
4.130
) at leading order, we use

{\mathcal{V}}_{N}^{\prime}/{\mathcal{V}}_{N}=-2S_{N}^{\prime}/\hbar
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime\prime}/{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}=S_{P}^{\prime\prime}/S_{P}^{\prime}-2S_{P}^{\prime}/\hbar
, giving

E_{2}=-\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}}{2}\left[\frac{2(S_{N}^{\prime}-S_{P}^{\prime})}{\hbar}+\frac{S_{P}^{\prime\prime}}{S_{P}^{\prime}}\right]\,.

(4.131)

The evaluation of
S^{\prime}_{N}
to leading order is obtained with Eq. (
4.111
) which gives
S^{\prime}_{N}=\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})-\ln(8/\hbar)+\cdots
. From Eq. (
4.108
),
S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})=\pi i(1+\tfrac{3}{2}\hbar\kappa+\cdots)
and
S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})=\pi i(\tfrac{3}{2}+\tfrac{105}{8}\hbar\kappa+\cdots)
. We then get

E_{2}=-\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}}{\hbar}\left[\Sigma+O(\hbar)\right]

(4.132)

where

\Sigma\equiv-\ln\frac{8}{\hbar}+\psi\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)-\pi i\,.

(4.133)

For the ground state (
\kappa=\frac{1}{2}
), using
\psi(1)=-\gamma_{E}
:

E_{2}\big|_{\kappa=1/2}=\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}}{\hbar}\left[\ln\frac{8}{\hbar}+\gamma_{E}+\pi i+O(\hbar)\right]\,.

(4.134)

Since
E_{1}
is real, the term
-\pi i
in
\Sigma
gives
E_{2}
a positive imaginary part:
\operatorname{Im}(E_{2})=+\pi(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}/\hbar
. This cancels against the imaginary ambiguity in the lateral Borel resummation of
E_{P}
, ensuring that the physical energy is real.

Higher instanton sectors

The expansion
E=E_{P}+\lambda E_{1}^{\pm}+\lambda^{2}E_{2}+\lambda^{3}E_{3}+\lambda^{4}E_{4}+\cdots
can be continued to arbitrary order in
\lambda
by straightforward Taylor expansion of the quantization condition. At each order
O(\lambda^{n+1})
, the equation is linear in
E_{n}
and can be solved algebraically once
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n-1}
are known. Define the log-derivative ratios

R_{N}\equiv\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}^{\prime}}{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}},\qquad R_{P}\equiv\frac{{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime\prime}}{{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}},

(4.135)

with all quantities evaluated at
E=E_{P}
. For example at
O(\lambda^{3})
we have
E_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}\,(R_{N}-R_{P})
as derived above. At
O(\lambda^{4})
, the quantization condition gives
E_{3}
as proportional to
(E_{1}^{\pm})^{3}
:

E_{3}=\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{3}}{24}\left[9R_{N}^{2}-18\,R_{N}R_{P}+8R_{P}^{2}+6R_{N}^{\prime}-4R_{P}^{\prime}\right]\,,

(4.136)

where
R_{N}^{\prime}=\partial_{E}R_{N}
and
R_{P}^{\prime}=\partial_{E}R_{P}
. Evaluating at leading order using
R_{N}=-2S_{N}^{\prime}/\hbar
,
R_{P}=S_{P}^{\prime\prime}/S_{P}^{\prime}-2S_{P}^{\prime}/\hbar
,
R_{N}^{\prime}=-2S_{N}^{\prime\prime}/\hbar
,
R_{P}^{\prime}=-2S_{P}^{\prime\prime}/\hbar+(S_{P}^{\prime\prime\prime}S_{P}^{\prime}-S_{P}^{\prime\prime 2})/S_{P}^{\prime 2}
, the 3-instanton correction becomes

E_{3}=\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{3}}{6\hbar^{2}}\left[9\Sigma^{2}+\pi^{2}-3\psi^{\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)+O(\hbar)\right]\,.

(4.137)

Since
E_{3}
depends on the sign of
E_{1}^{\pm}
, the 3-instanton sector contributes to the even/odd splitting as anticipated.

We can continue the expansion to higher orders. For instance, at
O(\lambda^{5})
, one finds
E_{4}
as a function of
(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}
and
(E_{1}^{\pm})^{4}
only:

\displaystyle E_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{4}}{24}\Big[8R_{N}^{3}-24\,R_{N}^{2}R_{P}+22\,R_{N}R_{P}^{2}-6R_{P}^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+12\,R_{N}R_{N}^{\prime}-8\,R_{N}R_{P}^{\prime}-12\,R_{N}^{\prime}R_{P}+7\,R_{P}R_{P}^{\prime}+2R_{N}^{\prime\prime}-R_{P}^{\prime\prime}\Big]

(4.138)

where
R_{N}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{E}^{2}R_{N}
and
R_{P}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{E}^{2}R_{P}
. At leading order we have:

E_{4}=-\frac{(E_{1}^{\pm})^{4}}{6\hbar^{3}}\left[16\Sigma^{3}+4\Sigma\pi^{2}-12\Sigma\,\psi^{\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)+\psi^{\prime\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)+O(\hbar)\right]\,.

(4.139)

Since
E_{4}
depends only on even powers of
E_{1}^{\pm}
, it is independent of the sign and contributes a common energy shift. This confirms the general pattern: odd instanton sectors (
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{3},\ldots
) produce splittings while even instanton sectors (
\lambda^{2},\lambda^{4},\ldots
) produce common shifts.

We emphasize that computing higher instanton corrections (higher powers of
\lambda
) at leading order in
\hbar
requires only Taylor expansion of the quantization condition followed by elementary algebra. In contrast, going to higher orders in
\hbar
within a single instanton sector is much harder: because of the
1/E^{2m-1}
poles in the Picard–Fuchs coefficients
a_{2m},b_{2m}
, every WKB order
P_{2m}
contributes at the same
\hbar
order when
E\sim\hbar
, and one must sum an entire series.

4.9
Alien calculus constraints on the trans-series

In the preceding sections we derived the trans-series
E=E_{P}+\lambda\,E_{1}^{\pm}+\lambda^{2}\,E_{2}+\cdots
by Taylor-expanding the quantization condition at each instanton order — the approach taken historically by Zinn-Justin
[
109
]
and refined by Zinn-Justin and Jentschura
[
107
,
108
]
and independently by Álvarez
[
3
]
for higher orders. Such calculations treat each instanton sector independently. However, the different sectors are related. The resurgent structure that connects perturbative and non-perturbative sectors and the
(1/2S_{I})^{n}\,n!
large-order growth of the perturbative coefficients, was made manifest in this context by Dunne and Ünsal
[
43
]
. A powerful tool for extracting the inter-sector relations systematically is the alien calculus of Écalle
[
47
,
48
,
49
]
. Alien calculus converts the DDP relation in Eq. (
4.87
),
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}(1+{\mathcal{V}}_{N})
, into an infinite tower of constraints relating the different instanton sectors and locating all Borel singularities in a unified framework.

The central objects in alien calculus are the
alien derivatives

\Delta_{n}
, introduced in Section
3.3
, which extract the Borel singularity at the
n
-instanton location
t=nS_{I}
. While
\Delta_{n}
extracts the monodromy around a single singularity,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}-1
gives the total monodromy around the positive real axis. The relationship between the two is
rather elegant
[
41
]
:

\boxed{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda^{n}\Delta_{n}\right)\,.}

(4.140)

To appreciate this formula, note that
\Delta_{n}
satisfies the Leibniz rule
\Delta_{n}(fg)=(\Delta_{n}f)\,g+f\,(\Delta_{n}g)
, so the alien derivatives are derivations, while
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
is a multiplicative automorphism which is the exponential of a derivation. So this is a Lie group relation: the group is the (infinite-dimensional) group of algebra automorphisms of the resurgent algebra, and its Lie algebra is the space of derivations, spanned by the
\Delta_{n}
with weights
\lambda^{n}=e^{-nS_{I}/\hbar}
fixed by the Borel singularity locations. The exponential map takes the derivation
\sum_{n}\lambda^{n}\Delta_{n}
in the Lie algebra to the Stokes automorphism
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}
in the group, and Eq. (
4.140
) is simply this exponential relation made explicit.

Expanding the exponential generates iterated alien derivatives. For example, the total discontinuity at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
receives contributions from both
\Delta_{2}
(intrinsic double-instanton singularity) and
\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{1}^{2}
(two successive single-instanton singularities). For the Airy function, we only had a single singularity at
t=2
, so
\Delta_{1}^{2}=0
and the exponential truncates:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}=1+e^{-2\zeta}\Delta_{1}
. For the double well, we can plug Écalle’s formula into the DDP relation
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}(1+{\mathcal{V}}_{N})
from Eq. (
4.87
) giving

\left(1+\lambda\Delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\Delta_{1}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\Delta_{2}+\cdots\right){\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}{\mathcal{V}}_{N}\,.

(4.141)

Matching terms at each order in
\lambda
will then give nontrivial constraints. The bulk of this section is occupied with computing the alien derivatives
\Delta_{n}E_{m}
sector by sector. Once this is done, we will be able to read off various resurgent relations and verify to a few non-trivial orders that the Borel-resummed spectrum is indeed real.

First alien derivative:
\Delta_{1}E_{n}

To compute the alien derivatives explicitly, since the object appearing in the quantization condition is
\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}=e^{-S_{N}/\hbar}\propto\lambda
it is helpful to define

\Upsilon(E)\;\equiv\;\frac{\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E)}}{\lambda}\;=\;e^{-(S_{N}(E)-S_{I})/\hbar}\;=\;\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma\!\left(\frac{E}{\hbar}+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{\!E/\hbar}+\cdots

(4.142)

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{N}={\mathcal{V}}_{N}
, the function
\Upsilon(E)
at
fixed

E
is Borel summable along the positive real axis:

\Delta_{n}[\Upsilon(E)]=0\qquad\text{for all }n\geq 1\,.

(4.143)

However, when
\Upsilon
is evaluated at
E=E_{P}
, the chain rule reintroduces a nontrivial alien derivative through the shift in
E_{P}
:

\Delta_{n}\bigl[\Upsilon(E_{P})\bigr]=\frac{\partial\Upsilon}{\partial E}\bigg|_{E_{P}}\Delta_{n}(E_{P})\,.

(4.144)

This distinction between fixed-
E
and on-shell (
E=E_{P}
) alien derivatives is crucial: any function of
E
that is Borel summable at fixed
E
acquires a nontrivial alien derivative when evaluated at
E_{P}
, because
E_{P}
itself has a Stokes jump.

Since
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}=\lambda^{2}\Upsilon^{2}
there is no
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
4.141
), so matching at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
gives

\Delta_{1}({\mathcal{V}}_{P})=0\,.

(4.145)

Since
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=e^{-2S_{P}/\hbar}
and
\Delta_{1}
is a derivation, we get immediately that
(\Delta_{1}S_{P})(E)=0
as a function of
E
. This is a statement about the period integrals in
S_{P}(E)
as an asymptotic series, independent of quantization.

The quantization condition at the perturbative level is
S_{P}(E_{P})=\pi i\kappa\hbar
with
\kappa=1/2+N
. The right-hand side is an exact polynomial in
\hbar
, so it has no Borel singularities and therefore neither does the left side:
\Delta_{1}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=0
. To determine
\Delta_{1}(E_{P})
we can use the chain rule:

\Delta_{1}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=\frac{\partial S_{P}}{\partial E}\bigg|_{E_{P}}\,\Delta_{1}(E_{P})+(\Delta_{1}S_{P})(E_{P})\,,

(4.146)

where
(\Delta_{1}S_{P})(E_{P})
denotes the alien derivative
\Delta_{1}S_{P}
(a function of
E
, computed at fixed
E
) evaluated at
E_{P}
. The left-hand side and the second term on the right vanish, and since generically
\partial_{E}S_{P}\neq 0
we conclude
\Delta_{1}(E_{P})=0
.

This result propagates to all sectors. To see the pattern, consider
E_{1}
: matching the quantization condition
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)=\pm i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E)}
at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
(Eq. (
4.120
)) gives

E_{1}^{\pm}=\pm\frac{i\,\Upsilon(E_{P})}{{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}\,,

(4.147)

where
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})=\partial_{E}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}|_{E_{P}}
arises from Taylor-expanding
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}
around
E_{P}
. Since
\Delta_{1}
commutes with
\partial_{E}
and annihilates
E_{P}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}(E)
, and
\Upsilon(E)
at fixed
E
, the chain rule gives
\Delta_{1}[\Upsilon(E_{P})]=\Delta_{1}[{\mathcal{V}}_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})]=0
, and Leibniz then gives
\Delta_{1}E_{1}=0
. The same argument iterates at every order: matching at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{n})
expresses
E_{n}
algebraically in
E_{P}
,
\Upsilon
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}
, their
E
-derivatives evaluated at
E_{P}
, and lower
E_{m<n}
, and
\Delta_{1}
annihilates each of these. Thus

\boxed{\Delta_{1}\,E_{n}=0\quad\text{for all }n\,.}

(4.148)

The nearest Borel singularity in every sector is therefore at
2S_{I}=\frac{4}{3}
(the instanton–anti-instanton action, not the single-instanton action
S_{I}
), so the asymptotic coefficients of every sector grow at worst as
(1/2S_{I})^{n}\,n!=(3/4)^{n}\,n!
. This is the first non-trivial result about the double well from the DDP formula and alien calculus.

Second alien derivative, perturbative:
\Delta_{2}E_{P}

Continuing the matching in Eq. (
4.141
), at order
\lambda^{2}
, since
\Delta_{1}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=0
is already established, we get

\Delta_{2}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\Upsilon^{2}\,.

(4.149)

It then follows that, at fixed
E
,
(\Delta_{2}S_{P})(E)=-(\hbar/2)\Upsilon^{2}(E)
. Separately, the quantization condition
S_{P}(E_{P})=\pi i\kappa\hbar
is an exact polynomial in
\hbar
(linear in
\hbar
and in
\kappa
), so
\Delta_{2}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=0
. The chain rule for
\Delta_{2}
on the composite
S_{P}(E_{P})
then gives

0=\Delta_{2}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})\,\Delta_{2}(E_{P})+(\Delta_{2}S_{P})(E_{P})\,,

(4.150)

and solving for
\Delta_{2}(E_{P})
gives

\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{2}}{2\,S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}

(4.151)

At leading order
S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})=\pi i+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
, so

\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=\frac{\hbar}{2\pi i}\Upsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(4.152)

This tells us that the leading Borel singularity of
E_{P}
is at
t=2S_{I}
with residue proportional to
\Upsilon^{2}
, and correspondingly that the perturbative coefficients of
E_{P}
grow at leading order as
(1/2S_{I})^{n}\,n!
.

Second alien derivative, non-perturbative:
\Delta_{2}E_{1}

The next quantity we want to compute is
\Delta_{2}(E_{1})
. For this, it is useful to introduce a shorthand at this point. Differentiating the quantization condition
S_{P}(E_{P}(\kappa,\hbar),\hbar)=\pi i\kappa\hbar
with respect to
\kappa
and using the chain rule gives
S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})\cdot\partial_{\kappa}E_{P}=\pi i\hbar
. Defining

F\equiv\partial_{\kappa}E_{P}=\frac{\pi i\hbar}{S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}

(4.153)

(which at leading order is
F=\hbar+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})
), Eq. (
4.151
) takes the compact form

\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=\frac{F\Upsilon^{2}}{2\pi i}\,.

(4.154)

From Eq. (
4.122
), the 1-instanton coefficient is
E_{1}=\pm F\Upsilon/(2\pi)
, so the Leibniz rule reduces
\Delta_{2}(E_{1})
to
\Delta_{2}(F)
and
\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)
. For
\Delta_{2}(F)
: since the Borel singularity at
t=2S_{I}=\tfrac{4}{3}
is fixed (independent of the quantum number),
\Delta_{2}
and
\partial_{\kappa}
commute,
[\Delta_{2},\partial_{\kappa}]=0
. Combined with Eq. (
4.151
),

\Delta_{2}(F)=\Delta_{2}(\partial_{\kappa}E_{P})=\partial_{\kappa}\,\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\,\partial_{\kappa}(F\Upsilon^{2})\,.

(4.155)

For
\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)
we note that since
\Upsilon
is Borel summable at fixed
E
, the on-shell alien derivative is given entirely by the chain rule, Eq. (
4.144
) at
n=2
,
\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)=\partial_{E}\Upsilon|_{E_{P}}\,\Delta_{2}(E_{P})
. At leading order, differentiating
\Upsilon=e^{-(S_{N}-S_{I})/\hbar}
gives
\partial_{E}\Upsilon=\Upsilon\,[\ln(8/\hbar)-\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})]/\hbar+\cdots
, so

\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)=\frac{F\Upsilon^{3}}{2\pi i\,\hbar}\left[\ln\tfrac{8}{\hbar}-\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})\right]+\cdots\,.

(4.156)

With the building blocks
\Delta_{2}(F)
and
\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)
in hand, computing
\Delta_{2}(E_{1})
is a direct application of the Leibniz rule to
E_{1}=\pm F\Upsilon/(2\pi)
:

\Delta_{2}(E_{1})=\pm\frac{1}{2\pi}\bigl[\Delta_{2}(F)\,\Upsilon+F\,\Delta_{2}(\Upsilon)\bigr].

(4.157)

Substituting Eqs. (
4.155
) and (
4.156
):

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}(E_{1})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\Upsilon}{4\pi^{2}i}\,\partial_{\kappa}(F\Upsilon^{2})\pm\frac{F^{2}\Upsilon^{3}}{4\pi^{2}i\hbar}\left[\log\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)-\psi\left(\kappa+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right]+\cdots

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\Upsilon}{4\pi^{2}i}\bigl[(\partial_{\kappa}F)\,\Upsilon^{2}+2F\Upsilon\,\partial_{\kappa}\Upsilon\bigr]\pm\frac{F^{2}\Upsilon^{3}}{4\pi^{2}i\hbar}\left[\log\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)-\psi\left(\kappa+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right]+\cdots\,.

(4.158)

From Eq. (
4.142
),
\partial_{\kappa}\ln\Upsilon=-\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})+\ln(8/\hbar)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
. Since
F=\hbar+\mathcal{O}(\kappa\hbar^{2})
and
\partial_{\kappa}F=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})
, the first term in the bracket is subleading. The leading-order result is then simply

\Delta_{2}(E_{1})=\pm\frac{3\hbar\,\Upsilon^{3}(-\psi(\kappa+\frac{1}{2})+\ln\frac{8}{\hbar})}{4\pi^{2}i}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(4.159)

Second alien derivative, higher non-perturbative:
\Delta_{2}E_{2}

Next we compute
\Delta_{2}(E_{2})
. From Eq. (
4.131
),
E_{2}=-(E_{1}^{\pm})^{2}[2(S_{N}^{\prime}-S_{P}^{\prime})/\hbar+S_{P}^{\prime\prime}/S_{P}^{\prime}]/2
with all derivatives evaluated at
E_{P}
. Applying the Leibniz rule requires, besides the already-computed
\Delta_{2}(E_{1})
, three more inputs:
\Delta_{2}[S_{N}^{\prime}(E_{P})]
,
\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})]
, and
\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})]
. We compute each via the chain rule.

For
\Delta_{2}[S_{N}^{\prime}(E_{P})]
: since
\Upsilon
is Borel summable at fixed
E
, so is
S_{N}^{\prime}
; hence
\Delta_{2}S_{N}^{\prime}|_{E}=0
and only the chain-rule term survives,

\Delta_{2}[S_{N}^{\prime}(E_{P})]=S_{N}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})\,\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=\frac{\Upsilon^{2}}{2\pi i}\,\psi^{\prime}(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})+\cdots,

(4.160)

using
S_{N}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})=\psi^{\prime}(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})/\hbar+\mathcal{O}(1)
at leading order.

For
\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})]
: both chain-rule terms contribute,

\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})]=S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})\,\Delta_{2}(E_{P})+\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime}|_{E}=-\Upsilon^{2}\left[\ln\tfrac{8}{\hbar}-\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})\right]+\cdots,

(4.161)

using
S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})=3\pi i/2+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
and
\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime}|_{E}=-\partial_{E}(\hbar\Upsilon^{2}/2)
(consistent with
\Delta_{2}S_{P}=-\hbar\Upsilon^{2}/2
); the
S_{P}^{\prime\prime}\,\Delta_{2}E_{P}
term is dropped as subleading in
\hbar
.

For
\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})]
: a similar chain-rule computation gives

\Delta_{2}[S_{P}^{\prime\prime}(E_{P})]=-\frac{\Upsilon^{2}}{\hbar}\left(2\left[\ln\tfrac{8}{\hbar}-\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})\right]^{2}-\psi^{\prime}(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})\right)+\cdots.

(4.162)

With these inputs (and
\Delta_{2}(E_{1}^{2})=2E_{1}\Delta_{2}(E_{1})
), the Leibniz rule applied to Eq. (
4.131
) gives

\Delta_{2}(E_{2})=-\frac{2E_{1}\Delta_{2}(E_{1})\Sigma}{\hbar}-\frac{E_{1}^{2}}{\hbar}\left(\Delta_{2}S_{N}^{\prime}-\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime}\right)-\frac{E_{1}^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{S_{P}^{\prime}\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime\prime}-S_{P}^{\prime\prime}\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime}}{(S_{P}^{\prime})^{2}}\right)\,,

(4.163)

where we used
E_{2}=-(E_{1})^{2}\Sigma/\hbar
in the first term (Eq. (
4.132
)). Each term is
\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
except
S_{P}^{\prime\prime}\Delta_{2}S_{P}^{\prime}
, which is
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})
. Substituting the leading-order expressions, using the definition of
\Sigma
from Eq. (
4.133
), and simplifying:

\Delta_{2}(E_{2})=\frac{i\hbar\Upsilon^{4}}{4\pi^{3}}\left[-4\left(\log\tfrac{8}{\hbar}-\psi\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right)^{2}-2i\pi\left(\log\tfrac{8}{\hbar}-\psi\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right)+\psi^{\prime}\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(4.164)

Higher alien derivatives

We now turn to the 4-instanton Borel singularity at
t=4S_{I}
, which is the
subleading
Borel singularity of the perturbative series. To locate it we compute
\Delta_{4}(E_{P})
.

Since
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}=\lambda^{2}\Upsilon^{2}
is
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
, the right-hand side of the DDP formula (
4.87
) has no contribution at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4})
. Expanding the left-hand side via Écalle’s formula (
4.140
) (with
\Delta_{1}=0
, only even alien derivatives contribute), the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4})
terms are the direct
\Delta_{4}
and the iterated
\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta_{2})^{2}
from the quadratic term of the exponential:

\Delta_{4}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\Delta_{2})^{2}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=0\,.

(4.165)

So
\Delta_{4}V_{P}
is determined by
(\Delta_{2})^{2}V_{P}
. Using
\Delta_{2}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{2}
(Eq. (
4.149
)) and
\Delta_{2}\Upsilon|_{E}=0
(Eq. (
4.143
)):

(\Delta_{2})^{2}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=\Delta_{2}({\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{2})=(\Delta_{2}{\mathcal{V}}_{P})\,\Upsilon^{2}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{4}\,,

(4.166)

giving
\Delta_{4}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{4}
at fixed
E
. Converting to
S_{P}
via
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=e^{-2S_{P}/\hbar}
gives
\Delta_{4}S_{P}|_{E}=\hbar\Upsilon^{4}/4
, and the chain rule with
\Delta_{4}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=0
(the quantization condition has no Borel singularity) gives

\Delta_{4}(E_{P})=-\frac{\Delta_{4}S_{P}|_{E}}{S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}=\frac{i\hbar\,\Upsilon^{4}}{4\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(4.167)

The nonzero (and purely imaginary)
\Delta_{4}(E_{P})
locates the subleading Borel singularity of
E_{P}
at
t=4S_{I}=\tfrac{8}{3}
, giving corrections that fall off as
(3/8)^{n}
relative to the leading
(3/4)^{n}
from
\Delta_{2}(E_{P})
.

For the imaginary-part cancellation at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4})
below, we will also need the
on-shell
value of
(\Delta_{2})^{2}(E_{P})
, which is the square-bracket version when
\Delta_{2}
acts with the chain rule rather than at fixed
E
. Applying
\Delta_{2}
to the on-shell expression
\Delta_{2}(E_{P})=F\Upsilon^{2}/(2\pi i)
via Leibniz, and using Eqs. (
4.155
), (
4.156
):

(\Delta_{2})^{2}(E_{P})=-\frac{\Upsilon^{2}\,\partial_{\kappa}(F\Upsilon^{2})}{4\pi^{2}}-\frac{F^{2}\Upsilon^{4}}{2\pi^{2}\hbar}\left[\log\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)-\psi\left(\kappa+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right]\,.

(4.168)

In contrast to
\Delta_{4}(E_{P})
, this is purely real. Also note that
\Delta_{2}(E_{2})
(Eq. (
4.164
)) locates the
leading
singularity of
E_{2}
at
t=2S_{I}
, confirming that
E_{2}
’s coefficients diverge at the same
(3/4)^{n}\,n!
rate as
E_{P}
’s.

Cancellation of imaginary parts

Reality of the physical energy requires the imaginary parts to cancel at each order in
\lambda
. At
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
, two terms contribute, from Eq. (
4.151
) and Eq. (
4.132
):

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}\bigl[\Delta_{2}(E_{P})\bigr]

\displaystyle=-\frac{F\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi}=-\frac{\hbar\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi}+\cdots\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\bigl[E_{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{1}^{2}}{\hbar}\pi=\frac{\hbar\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi}+\cdots\,,

(4.169)

where we used
F=\hbar+O(\hbar^{2})
and
E_{1}=F\Upsilon/2\pi
. The imaginary part of
E_{2}
indeed cancels against the imaginary part incurred in the Borel resummation of the leading singularity in
E_{P}
.

At
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4})
, three terms contribute, from Eq. (
4.167
), Eq. (
4.164
) and Eq. (
4.139
). Writing
\Sigma_{R}\equiv\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})-\ln(8/\hbar)
(the real part of
\Sigma
), they are

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}\bigl[\Delta_{4}(E_{P})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Upsilon^{4}}{8\pi^{3}}\pi^{2}+\cdots\,,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}\bigl[\Delta_{2}(E_{2})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Upsilon^{4}}{8\pi^{3}}\left(\psi^{\prime}-4\Sigma_{R}^{2}\right)+\cdots\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\bigl[E_{4}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Upsilon^{4}}{8\pi^{3}}\bigl(4\Sigma_{R}^{2}-\pi^{2}-\psi^{\prime}\bigr)+\cdots\,.

(4.170)

Their sum vanishes identically, confirming reality of
E
within the formal Borel framework. The same cancellation will occur at all higher orders in
\lambda
.

All-orders structure

The results above generalize to all
\Delta_{2n}
and
E_{n}
. First, the bridge pattern:
\Delta_{2}
maps each
E_{n}
to something proportional to
\Upsilon^{n+2}
, connecting it to the
(n+2)
-instanton level:

\Delta_{2}\,E_{n}\;\propto\;\Upsilon^{n+2}\;\longleftrightarrow\;E_{n+2}\,,\qquad\Delta_{1}\,E_{n}=0\quad\text{for all }n\,.

(4.171)

The even sublattice (
E_{P},E_{2},E_{4},\ldots
) and odd sublattice (
E_{1},E_{3},E_{5},\ldots
) are completely disconnected: no sequence of alien derivatives bridges them. This is the resurgent explanation for why the level splitting, which lives entirely in the odd sublattice, is invisible to the large-order behavior of perturbation theory.

Second, the general formula for
\Delta_{2n}V_{P}
. For
n\geq 2
, expanding the DDP formula to
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2n})
gives the recursion

\Delta_{2n}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+k=n\\
j,k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Delta_{2j}\,\Delta_{2k}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}+\cdots=0\,.

(4.172)

Since
\Delta_{2n}(\Upsilon)=0
at fixed
E
(Eq. (
4.143
)) and
\Delta_{2}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\Upsilon^{2}
, repeated application of the Leibniz rule gives
(\Delta_{2})^{n}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{2n}
, and the combinatorics of the exponential reduce to

\Delta_{2n}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{2n}\,,

(4.173)

which for
n=1
reproduces
\Delta_{2}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}={\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{2}
and for
n=2
reproduces
\Delta_{4}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathcal{V}}_{P}\,\Upsilon^{4}
. Converting to
E_{P}
using
V_{P}=e^{-2S_{P}/\hbar}
and the chain rule from
\Delta_{2n}[S_{P}(E_{P})]=0
:

\Delta_{2n}(E_{P})=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{2\pi i\,n}\,F\,\Upsilon^{2n}\,.

(4.174)

This result exhibits the rich structure within the perturbative series. All even alien derivatives act non-trivially on
E_{P}
, giving Borel poles at every
t=2nS_{I}
. Since
[\Delta_{2n},\,\partial_{\kappa}]=0
, the analog of Eq. (
4.155
) is
\Delta_{2n}(F)=(-1)^{n+1}\partial_{\kappa}(F\Upsilon^{2n})/(2\pi i\,n)
, and Eq. (
4.174
) resums into a remarkably compact form:

\boxed{\;\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda^{2n}\,\Delta_{2n}(E_{P})\;=\;\frac{F}{2\pi i}\,\ln(1+{\mathcal{V}}_{N})\,.\;}

(4.175)

This is arguably the central result of the section. It packages
every
Borel singularity of
E_{P}
, residues at all
t=2nS_{I}
,
n\geq 1
, into a single logarithm of the local object
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E)=e^{-2S_{N}(E)/\hbar}
.

One might hope to exponentiate Eq. (
4.175
) via Écalle’s bridge
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}=\exp(\sum_{n}\lambda^{n}\Delta_{n})
and obtain a closed form for the total Stokes discontinuity
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}-1)E_{P}
, the ambiguity between the lateral Borel resummations
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}E_{P}
. But
D=\sum_{n}\lambda^{n}\Delta_{n}
is a derivation, not a function: expanding
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}=1+D+\tfrac{1}{2}D^{2}+\cdots
gives
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}-1)E_{P}=DE_{P}+\tfrac{1}{2}D^{2}E_{P}+\cdots
, where
D^{k}E_{P}
means applying
D
iteratively, not powers of
DE_{P}
. The resulting terms from iterated derivations drag in iterated alien derivatives such as
\Delta_{2m}\Delta_{2n}E_{P}
, carrying data (
\partial_{\kappa}F
,
\partial_{E}\Upsilon
,
\psi(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2})
,
\ln\hbar
) that does not repackage as a function of
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}
alone. Only at leading order in
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}
(equivalently
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
) does the iteration drop out, leaving

({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{\mathcal{S}}}-1)\,E_{P}\;=\;\frac{\hbar\,{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}{2\,S_{P}^{\prime}(E_{P})}+\mathcal{O}({\mathcal{V}}_{N}^{2})\,,

(4.176)

which is the imaginary ambiguity of the resummed perturbative series at leading instanton order.

4.10
Summary of the double well trans-series

The Exact WKB quantization condition determines the energy levels of the double-well potential as a trans-series in the tunneling factor
\lambda=e^{-S_{I}/\hbar}
where
S_{I}=2/3
:

E=E_{P}+\lambda\,E_{1}^{\pm}+\lambda^{2}\,E_{2}+\lambda^{3}\,E_{3}^{\pm}+\lambda^{4}\,E_{4}+\cdots

(4.177)

where
\kappa=N+\frac{1}{2}
for the
N
-th energy level, and the
\pm
distinguishes even and odd parity states. Odd instanton sectors (
E_{1},E_{3},\ldots
) are proportional to odd powers of
E_{1}^{\pm}
and produce splittings between even and odd states. Even instanton sectors (
E_{2},E_{4},\ldots
) depend on even powers of
E_{1}^{\pm}
and produce common energy shifts. The first few sectors are:

\displaystyle E_{P}

\displaystyle=\hbar\kappa-\hbar^{2}\frac{1+12\kappa^{2}}{16}-\hbar^{3}\frac{19\kappa+68\kappa^{3}}{64}-\hbar^{4}\frac{131+3672\kappa^{2}+6000\kappa^{4}}{2048}+\cdots

(4.178)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon}{2\pi}\,\bigl(1+O(\hbar)\bigr)

(4.179)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\,\Sigma\,\bigl(1+O(\hbar)\bigr)

(4.180)

\displaystyle E_{3}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{3}}{48\pi^{3}}\left[9\Sigma^{2}+\pi^{2}-3\psi^{\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right]\bigl(1+O(\hbar)\bigr)

(4.181)

\displaystyle E_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{4}}{96\pi^{4}}\left[16\Sigma^{3}+4\Sigma\pi^{2}-12\Sigma\,\psi^{\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)+\psi^{\prime\prime}\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right]\bigl(1+O(\hbar)\bigr)

(4.182)

where

\Upsilon(E_{P})\;=\;\frac{\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}(E_{P})}}{\lambda}\;=\;\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma\!\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)^{\!\kappa}\bigl(1+O(\hbar)\bigr)\,,

(4.183)

is the perturbative fluctuation factor evaluated at
E=E_{P}
, and

\Sigma\;\equiv\;\Sigma_{R}-i\pi\,,\quad\Sigma_{R}=-\ln\left(\frac{8}{\hbar}\right)+\psi\left(\kappa+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(4.184)

with
\psi(z)=\Gamma^{\prime}(z)/\Gamma(z)
the digamma function.

Alien calculus (Section
4.9
) reveals the resurgent structure underlying these series. The alien derivative
\Delta_{n}
probes the Borel singularity at
t=nS_{I}
, extracting the connection between sectors. A key result is

\Delta_{m}\,E_{n}=0\quad\text{for}\penalty\ m\penalty\ \text{odd and all}\penalty\ n\,.

(4.185)

This tells us that the even sublattice (
E_{P},E_{2},E_{4},\ldots
) and odd sublattice (
E_{1},E_{3},E_{5},\ldots
) are completely disconnected: no sequence of alien derivatives bridges them. Every sector can have its nearest Borel singularity at most at
2S_{I}=\frac{4}{3}
(the instanton–anti-instanton action), so all sectors diverge at most as
(3/4)^{n}\,n!
.

The perturbative series has even alien derivatives

\Delta_{2n}(E_{P})=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{2\pi i\,n}\,F\,\Upsilon^{2n}\,.

(4.186)

where
F\equiv\partial_{\kappa}E_{P}
. This means it has singularities in the Borel plane at
2nS_{I}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
. The first nonzero alien derivative
\Delta_{2}E_{P}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}F\Upsilon^{2}
(Eq. (
4.151
)) gives the leading imaginary part of the Borel resummed
E_{P}
; this is the imaginary part of
E_{2}
in the trans-series. Alien derivatives of the series of the non-perturbative corrections to the energy can also be computed systematically:

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}(E_{1})

\displaystyle=\mp\frac{3\hbar\,\Upsilon^{3}\Sigma_{R}}{4\pi^{2}i}+\cdots\,,

(4.187)

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}(E_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar\Upsilon^{4}}{4\pi^{3}}\left[-4\Sigma_{R}^{2}+2i\pi\Sigma_{R}+\psi^{\prime}\left(\kappa+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right]+\cdots

(4.188)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{2})^{2}(E_{P})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Upsilon^{2}\,\partial_{\kappa}(F\Upsilon^{2})}{4\pi^{2}}+\frac{\Upsilon^{2}F\,\Sigma_{R}}{2\pi^{2}}+\cdots\,,

(4.189)

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}(E_{P})

\displaystyle=-\frac{F\,\Upsilon^{4}}{4\pi i}+\cdots\,.

(4.190)

Non-trivial checks of the formalism are the cancellations of the imaginary parts of the energy at each order in
\lambda
, which guarantee that the physical spectrum is real. At
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
, the imaginary part of
E_{2}
cancels against the imaginary ambiguity of the Borel resummation of
E_{P}
:

\operatorname{Im}[E_{2}]\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}[\Delta_{2}E_{P}]\;=\;\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi}\;-\;\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{2}}{4\pi}\;=\;0\,.

(4.191)

At
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4})
, three contributions must conspire to cancel (with
(\Delta_{2})^{2}E_{P}
purely real at leading order and hence not contributing):

\operatorname{Im}[E_{4}]\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}[\Delta_{2}E_{2}]\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}[\Delta_{4}E_{P}]\;=\;\frac{\hbar\,\Upsilon^{4}}{8\pi^{3}}\bigl[(4\Sigma_{R}^{2}-\pi^{2}-\psi^{\prime})+(\psi^{\prime}-4\Sigma_{R}^{2})+\pi^{2}\bigr]\;=\;0\,.

(4.192)

5
Euclidean Path Integral

The Exact WKB approach is a systematic procedure to compute the energy levels of the double well. The quantization condition is encoded in the relation
1+{\mathcal{V}}_{P}=\pm i\sqrt{{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}
where
{\mathcal{V}}_{P}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{N}
are the Voros symbols given by period integrals over the quantum action. Solving this quantization condition perturbatively leads to a trans-series for the energies. Although Exact WKB can in principle be used to compute the energies to arbitrary order, the quantization condition makes it hard to see the origin of the resurgent structure. An alternative approach to computing the same trans-series involves the Euclidean Feynman path integral. With the path integral, each term in the trans-series for the partition function
Z
comes from a perturbative calculation around a different saddle. One can then see the resurgent structure in the partition function directly, and independent of the energy quantization step. Although the resurgent structure is easier to see, a drawback of the path integral approach is that one must work at finite
T
where elliptic functions abound. The path integral thereby gives a complementary view of the double well compared with Exact WKB.

5.1
Partition function basics

The energy levels in quantum mechanics are encoded in the partition function

Z=\sum_{N}e^{-TE^{(N)}(\hbar)/\hbar}=\sum_{N}\langle N|e^{-HT/\hbar}|N\rangle={\operatorname{Tr}}(e^{-HT/\hbar})\,.

(5.1)

For example, the ground state energy can be extracted with

E^{(0)}=-\hbar\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\ln Z\,.

(5.2)

Since the symmetric double well is invariant under parity
\mathcal{P}:x\to-x
, the energy eigenstates are parity eigenstates. They split into even and odd parity pairs
E^{(N)}_{\pm}
. To extract the splitting it is helpful to introduce the twisted partition function

\widetilde{Z}={\operatorname{Tr}}\left(\mathcal{P}e^{-HT/\hbar}\right)=\sum_{N}\left(e^{-E^{(N)}_{+}T/\hbar}-e^{-E^{(N)}_{-}T/\hbar}\right)\,.

(5.3)

Then, at small energy differences,

\widetilde{Z}=-\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{N}e^{-E^{(N)}_{+}T/\hbar}(E^{(N)}_{+}-E^{(N)}_{-})T+\cdots

(5.4)

which allows us to extract the splittings. To compute
Z
and
\widetilde{Z}
with the path integral we begin with the Euclidean propagator

\langle b|e^{-HT/\hbar}|a\rangle=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(0)=a}^{x(T)=b}\mathcal{D}xe^{-S[x]/\hbar}\,.

(5.5)

Taking the trace in position space gives

Z=\int da\langle a|e^{-HT/\hbar}|a\rangle=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(0)=x(T)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{D}xe^{-S[x]/\hbar}\,,

(5.6)

while the twisted trace gives

\widetilde{Z}=\int da\langle-a|e^{-HT/\hbar}|a\rangle=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(T)=-x(0)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{D}xe^{-S[x]/\hbar}\,.

(5.7)

So
Z
sums periodic paths and
\widetilde{Z}
sums anti-periodic paths in Euclidean time.

From
E
trans-series to
Z
trans-series

Both the partition functions and the energies are trans-series in
\hbar
. Using
\lambda=e^{-S_{I}/\hbar}
with
S_{I}=2/3
as in Exact WKB, we can write these trans-series as

E^{(N)}(\hbar)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\lambda^{k}E_{k}^{(N)}(\hbar),\qquad Z(T,\hbar)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\lambda^{k}Z_{k}(T,\hbar)\,.

(5.8)

Each
E_{k}^{(N)}(\hbar)
is an asymptotic series in
\hbar
and
\ln\hbar
. Throughout the path-integral analysis we use
E_{k}^{(N)}
with the subscript
k
denoting the instanton sector (matching the convention of Section
4
) and the parenthesized superscript
(N)
the state index. We drop the state superscript when working with the ground state, so
E_{k}\equiv E_{k}^{(0)}
. Expanding the exponential in the small
\lambda
regime, using Eq. (
5.1
) and matching powers of
\lambda
gives

\displaystyle Z_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}e^{-TE_{0}^{(N)}/\hbar},

(5.9)

\displaystyle Z_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}e^{-TE_{0}^{(N)}/\hbar}\!\left[-\frac{T}{\hbar}E_{1}^{(N)}\right],

(5.10)

\displaystyle Z_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}e^{-TE_{0}^{(N)}/\hbar}\!\left[-\frac{T}{\hbar}E_{2}^{(N)}+\frac{T^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\bigl(E_{1}^{(N)}\bigr)^{2}\right],

(5.11)

\displaystyle Z_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}e^{-TE_{0}^{(N)}/\hbar}\!\left[-\frac{T}{\hbar}E_{3}^{(N)}+\frac{T^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}E_{1}^{(N)}E_{2}^{(N)}-\frac{T^{3}}{6\hbar^{3}}\bigl(E_{1}^{(N)}\bigr)^{3}\right]

(5.12)

\displaystyle Z_{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}e^{-TE_{0}^{(N)}/\hbar}\!\left[-\frac{T}{\hbar}E_{4}^{(N)}+\frac{T^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\left(E_{1}^{(N)}E_{3}^{(N)}+\frac{1}{2}\bigl(E_{2}^{(N)}\bigr)^{2}\right)-\frac{T^{3}}{2\hbar^{3}}\bigl(E_{1}^{(N)}\bigr)^{2}E_{2}^{(N)}+\frac{T^{4}}{24\hbar^{4}}\bigl(E_{1}^{(N)}\bigr)^{4}\right]\,.

(5.13)

These formulas are misleadingly simple. Because
E_{0}^{(N)}
is a series in
\hbar
, the series for each
Z_{k}(T,\hbar)
will actually mix different perturbative orders in the energies as well as different elements of the trans-series. In addition, due to parity invariance,
Z_{2m+1}=0
(in particular
Z_{1}=0
), consistent with the absence of odd-instanton periodic saddles. This does
not
imply vanishing one-instanton energy shifts in each parity sector, but it does imply that one-instanton energy shifts come with opposite signs for pairs of even and odd energy states. Indeed
E_{1}^{(N)}
is not zero, and in fact its square shows up in the
T^{2}
term of
Z_{2}
. As we will see,
E_{1}^{(N)}
is easier to extract from the twisted partition function
\widetilde{Z}
where it appears at order
\lambda
without mixing with other orders.

Another method for extracting the energies from the partition function is through its Laplace transform, called the
resolvent
:

G(E)=\frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{0}^{\infty}dT\,e^{ET/\hbar}Z=\sum_{N}\frac{1}{E^{(N)}-E}={\operatorname{Tr}}\frac{1}{H-E}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial E}\ln D(E)

(5.14)

where the Fredholm (spectral) determinant is
D(E)=\det(H-E)
. The energies
E^{(N)}

are then the poles of
G(E)
or zeros of
D(E)
.

Path integral basis and the SHO

The normalization
\mathcal{N}
of the path integral is formally infinite. But it is a property of the measure, independent of the action, and we can therefore fix it in the free theory and apply it everywhere. Fourier modes provide a convenient basis for periodic paths:

x(t)=c_{0}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}c_{n}\psi_{n},\quad\psi_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}e^{i\omega_{n}t},\qquad\omega_{n}=\frac{2\pi n}{T}

(5.15)

Reality means
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
c_{-n}=c_{n}^{*}
for
n\geq 1
. These modes are eigenfunctions of
-\partial_{t}^{2}
with eigenvalues
\omega_{n}^{2}
, so the free action is diagonal:

S_{0}[x]=\int_{0}^{T}\!dt\,\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\omega_{n}^{2}\,|c_{n}|^{2}\,.

(5.16)

The constant offset is a zero mode with
\omega_{0}=0
and the action does not depend on
c_{0}
. So the free path integral is

Z_{\text{free}}=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(0)=x(T)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{D}x\,e^{-S_{0}[x]/\hbar}=\mathcal{N}\int_{0}^{L}dc_{0}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dc_{n}dc_{n}^{*}e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}\omega_{n}^{2}\,|c_{n}|^{2}}=\mathcal{N}\,L\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\pi\hbar}{\omega_{n}^{2}}\,,

(5.17)

where the limits of the offset
c_{0}
are fixed by the allowed range of
x
. Comparing to the spectral computation of the free-particle partition function

Z_{\text{free}}={\operatorname{Tr}}(e^{-H_{0}T/\hbar})=\int_{0}^{L}dx\langle x|e^{-H_{0}T/\hbar}|x\rangle=\int_{0}^{L}dx\int\frac{dp}{2\pi\hbar}\langle x|e^{-\frac{p^{2}}{2}T/\hbar}|p\rangle\langle p|x\rangle=L\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar T}}\,,

(5.18)

fixes the normalization

\mathcal{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar T}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{n}^{2}}{\pi\hbar}\,.

(5.19)

This normalization can now be used for any action.

For the SHO with
V=\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
the same Fourier modes are eigenmodes of
-\partial_{t}^{2}+V^{\prime\prime}(0)=-\partial_{t}^{2}+1
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=\omega_{n}^{2}+1
. In the basis of Eq. (
5.15
) the action is
S_{\text{SHO}}=\frac{T}{2}c_{0}^{2}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}\,|c_{n}|^{2}
, where only the zero mode picks up an explicit
T
from
\int_{0}^{T}x^{2}\,dt
. Since
\lambda_{0}=1
, the zero mode is lifted and the
c_{0}
integral becomes Gaussian, giving
\sqrt{2\pi\hbar/T}
. Each
n\geq 1
pair contributes
\pi\hbar/\lambda_{n}
, so we get

Z_{\text{SHO}}=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(0)=x(T)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{D}x\,e^{-S_{\text{SHO}}[x]/\hbar}=\frac{1}{T}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{n}^{2}}{\omega_{n}^{2}+1}=\frac{1}{T}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+\frac{T^{2}}{4\pi^{2}n^{2}}}=\frac{1}{2\sinh\frac{T}{2}}\,,

(5.20)

Comparing to the spectral computation, with
E^{(N)}=\hbar(N+\frac{1}{2})
,

Z_{\text{SHO}}=\sum_{N}e^{-(N+\frac{1}{2})T}=\frac{1}{2\sinh\frac{T}{2}}

(5.21)

in agreement with Eq. (
5.20
).

5.2
Complex instantons for the double well

Now we turn to the double well with potential
V(x)=\frac{1}{8}(x^{2}-1)^{2}
. This potential has two classical minima and the system has two classical static solutions:
x(t)=\pm 1
. We call these the perturbative saddles. Expanding around them gives the zero-instanton sector of the trans-series, with no
\exp(-S_{I}/\hbar)
suppression. The rest of the trans-series comes from expanding around other non-trivial complex classical solutions to the equations of motion. Our next task is to find all such solutions. After that, we need to understand which solutions contribute to the partition function, and how.

5.2.1
Elliptic Curves

The partition function is a path integral in Euclidean time and the corresponding Lagrangian
L=\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}^{2}+V(x)
is Euclidean. The saddle points are then solutions to

\ddot{x}+U^{\prime}(x)=0

(5.22)

where

U(x)=-V(x)=-\frac{1}{8}(x^{2}-1)^{2}

(5.23)

is the inverted potential. Using
U(x)
instead of
V(x)
gives us physical intuition for the (real) solutions: they are ordinary trajectories of a particle moving in the potential
U(x)
. Since the potential is time-independent there is a conserved energy and the solutions all satisfy

\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}^{2}+U(x)=\varepsilon\,.

(5.24)

Here
\varepsilon
is the energy of the classical Euclidean solutions which is not the same as a quantum energy
E
of a double-well energy eigenstate. We can solve Eq. (
5.24
) by integration. There are two roots
\dot{x}=\pm\sqrt{2\varepsilon-2U(x)}
. Integrating them gives an implicit solution for
x(t)

t=\pm\int^{x(t)}_{x(0)}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon-2U(x^{\prime})}}\,.

(5.25)

These are elliptic integrals.

Rather than attempt to integrate (
5.25
) directly, we employ a precise change of variables. We first find the 4 roots
x_{j}
of
U(x)=\varepsilon
. These are turning points of the classical motion in the inverted well. For any fixed
x_{j}
, we can then change variables from
x(t)
to
\wp(t)
as

x(t)=x_{j}\left[1+\frac{6(x_{j}^{2}-1)}{1-3x_{j}^{2}+24\wp(t-t_{0})}\right]\,.

(5.26)

Then (
5.24
) becomes the
Weierstrass equation
:

\dot{\wp}^{2}=4\wp^{3}-g_{2}\wp-g_{3},

(5.27)

with

g_{2}=\frac{6\varepsilon+1}{12},\quad g_{3}=-\frac{9\varepsilon+1}{216}\,.

(5.28)

The solutions are the
Weierstrass functions

\wp(t)
defined implicitly from

t=\int\frac{dt}{d\wp}d\wp=\int_{\infty}^{\wp}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{\sqrt{4x^{\prime 3}-g_{2}x^{\prime}-g_{3}}}\,.

(5.29)

Although these functions do not depend on which turning point
x_{j}
we chose, any
\wp(t)
will generate four solutions
x(t)
from Eq. (
5.26
). Note also that the Weierstrass functions are conventionally defined with this definite integral; the integration constant is the global time-translation modulus encoded as
t_{0}
in Eq. (
5.26
).

Weierstrass functions are central objects in the study of elliptic curves. An
elliptic curve
is defined as the locus of solutions to
y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}
over some field. Over
\mathbb{R}
, the curve describes motion along a cubic potential. For quantum mechanics, we are interested in elliptic curves over
\mathbb{C}
which have a remarkable property: the motion is doubly periodic. For a cubic potential, one period is the harmonic oscillation around the minimum. The other period is the harmonic oscillation around the maximum (equivalently the minimum of
V(x)
). This double periodicity makes elliptic curves have the topology of a torus, i.e. they are genus 1. Even though the double well is quartic, because it is symmetric it has only two independent periods and is also genus 1. Alternatively, the map in Eq. (
5.26
) maps the quartic equation for the double well directly to a cubic. The periods for the double well are shown in Fig.
10
.

\varepsilon
\omega_{P}
\omega_{N}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{4}

\varepsilon
\omega_{P}
\omega_{N}

y=0
\omega
\omega^{\prime}
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}

Figure 10:
Left: oscillation of a particle with energy
\varepsilon
in a double well potential has one real “perturbative” half-period
\omega_{P}
and one imaginary non-perturbative half-period
\omega_{N}
. Center: in the Euclidean path integral, the potential is inverted and
\omega_{P}
and
\omega_{N}
are swapped, so
\omega_{P}
is imaginary and
\omega_{N}
is real. Right: the double well maps to an elliptic curve (a cubic potential) with half-periods
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
.

A starting point for building intuition for elliptic curves is to start with a simpler case of a genus 0 curve: the circle. The circle comprises solutions to
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
, namely
y=\pm\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
. We can integrate
dx/y
by writing
x
and
y
as functions of a parameter
t
along the curve. More precisely, we want to find a parameterization such that
y(t)=\partial_{t}x(t)
. Then the circle equation maps to the differential equation
\dot{x}^{2}+x^{2}=1
which is the genus-0 analog of the Weierstrass equation. We can find the parameterization by integration

t=\int dt=\int\frac{dt}{dx}dx=\int\frac{dx}{y}=\int^{x}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}=\sin^{-1}x\,.

(5.30)

This leads to
x=\sin t
and
y=\dot{x}=\cos t
, as one might have guessed. Eq. (
5.30
) is the analog of the period integral in Eqs. (
5.25
) or (
5.29
). If we integrate between the turning points where
y=0
we get

\omega_{\operatorname{circle}}=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}=\pi\,,

(5.31)

which is, as expected, the half-period of the circle.

The elliptic curve equation
y^{2}=4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}
is related to Eq. (
5.27
) by the parameterization
x=\wp(t)
and
y=\dot{x}=\wp^{\prime}(t)
. The turning points correspond to no kinetic energy
\dot{x}=y=0
. Denoting the turning points by
e_{j}
these must satisfy
4e_{j}^{3}-g_{2}e_{j}-g_{3}=0
. In the original double well, there are 4 turning points where
\varepsilon=U(x)
and
\dot{x}=0
, but due to the
x\to-x
symmetry only two are independent. The double well and its cubic image are shown in Fig.
10
. Consider the cubic case on the right. A particle can start at rest with energy
\varepsilon
at the central turning point
e_{2}
, roll down to
e_{3}
, reverse direction, and return to
e_{2}
after one period. The complementary cycle is reached by analytic continuation
t\to it
, which exchanges the two motions. In the Euclidean conventions used below, the first cycle corresponds to
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
and the second to
\omega^{\prime}\in i\mathbb{R}
. The two half-periods are

\omega^{\prime}=\int_{e_{1}}^{e_{2}}\frac{dx}{y(x)},\quad\omega=\int_{e_{2}}^{e_{3}}\frac{dx}{y(x)},\qquad y(x)=\sqrt{4x^{3}-g_{2}x-g_{3}}\,,

(5.32)

where the square-root branch is chosen consistently on the corresponding sheet of the elliptic curve. Choosing the opposite branch
y\to-y
flips the sign of both periods but does not change the period lattice. For the double well the half-periods are exactly the integrals between the classical turning points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
:

\omega_{P}=\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon-2U(x)}},\qquad\omega_{N}=\int_{x_{2}}^{x_{3}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{2\varepsilon-2U(x)}},

(5.33)

as in Eq. (
5.25
). With these conventions, for
-\frac{1}{8}<\varepsilon<0
,
\omega_{P}
is imaginary and
\omega_{N}
is real.
\omega_{P}
is
i
times the classical half-period for motion in the double well potential
V(x)
, and
\omega_{N}
is the classical half-period in the inverted double well
U(x)
.

Note that these integrals are the same period integrals that surfaced in Section
4.7
in the Exact WKB approach. There
P_{0}=\sqrt{2V-2E}
with
V=-U
, and
E=-\varepsilon
matching to the Euclidean energy convention. Thus
\omega_{P}=-\partial_{E}S_{P}^{0}
evaluated at
E=-\varepsilon
. The explicit forms for these periods are

\omega_{P}(\varepsilon)=-\frac{2i}{\sqrt{1+\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}}}K\left(\frac{4\sqrt{-2\varepsilon}}{1+\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}}\right),\quad\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\sqrt{8}}{\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}}}K\left(\frac{1+8\varepsilon-\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}}{4\varepsilon}\right)

(5.34)

with
K
the Elliptic
K
function. These have equivalent hypergeometric forms given by
\partial_{E}
acting on Eqs. (
4.93
) and (
4.97
) with
E=-\varepsilon
. The dependence of these periods on
\varepsilon
is shown in Fig.
11
.

The Weierstrass functions usually written as
\wp(t;\omega,\omega^{\prime})
have a number of useful properties. They are doubly periodic with half periods
\omega
and

\omega^{\prime}
. This means that

\wp(t;\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\wp(t+2\omega;\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\wp(t+2\omega^{\prime};\omega,\omega^{\prime})\,.

(5.35)

They are even functions
\wp(t)=\wp(-t)
, which follows from time-reversal invariance of the Weierstrass equation. They are meromorphic functions with double poles at all lattice points
t=2k\omega+2k^{\prime}\omega^{\prime}
for any
k,k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Near a pole
t_{\star}
, the local behavior is

\wp(t)=\frac{1}{(t-t_{\star})^{2}}+\frac{g_{2}}{20}(t-t_{\star})^{2}+\frac{g_{3}}{28}(t-t_{\star})^{4}+\cdots

(5.36)

so there is no simple-pole term. At the half periods, the Weierstrass functions are finite and, for this labeling convention, give the turning points:

\wp(\omega;\omega,\omega^{\prime})=e_{1},\quad\wp(\omega^{\prime};\omega,\omega^{\prime})=e_{3},\quad\wp(\omega+\omega^{\prime};\omega,\omega^{\prime})=e_{2}\,.

(5.37)

The functions have a scaling law

\wp(\lambda t;\lambda\omega,\lambda\omega^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\wp(t;\omega,\omega^{\prime})

(5.38)

so that

\omega^{2}e_{1}=\omega^{2}\wp(\omega;\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\wp(1;1,\tau)\,,

(5.39)

where
\tau=\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{\omega}
is the modular parameter of the elliptic curve.

\varepsilon
\omega_{P}
\omega_{N}
U(x)
0
-\tfrac{1}{8}
x
-1
0
1

Figure 11:
Left: the inverted potential
U(x)=-\frac{1}{8}(x^{2}-1)^{2}
for the double well. Choosing a real energy in the range
-\frac{1}{8}<\varepsilon<0
, the perturbative period
\omega_{P}
is imaginary and the non-perturbative period
\omega_{N}
is real.
\operatorname{Im}\omega_{P}
and
\operatorname{Re}\omega_{N}
are shown on the right. As
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
we approach the instanton solution going between the two turning points so that the non-perturbative period gets large and
\omega_{P}\rightarrow-\pi i
.

5.2.2
Periodic saddles

The generic solutions to the Euclidean equations of motion for the double well are written in Eq. (
5.26
) in terms of Weierstrass functions
\wp(t;\omega,\omega^{\prime})
and the choice of one of the four turning points
x_{j}
. To restrict these solutions to saddle points of the partition function, we must next impose periodic boundary conditions
x(t)=x(t+T)
. Conveniently, the solutions to the double well inherit periodicity from the Weierstrass functions, as in Eq. (
5.35
), with half-periods given explicitly in Eq. (
5.33
). So all the generic double-well solutions are periodic under

t\rightarrow t+2k\omega_{N}+2k^{\prime}\omega_{P}\,.

(5.40)

Here
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
label how many times the trajectory winds around the two primitive period directions of the Weierstrass solution,
2\omega_{N}
and
2\omega_{P}
. In terms of period integrals,
k
and
k^{\prime}
enumerate the homology class of the integration contour.

For a solution to be periodic in time
T
we must then have

2k\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)+2k^{\prime}\omega_{P}(\varepsilon)=T\,,

(5.41)

for some integers
k,k^{\prime}
. This is only solved at quantized values of the classical energy
\varepsilon
. Since
\omega_{N}
is real and
\omega_{P}
imaginary for real
\varepsilon
, the quantization condition forces
k^{\prime}=0
for real
\varepsilon
; nonzero
k^{\prime}
requires complex
\varepsilon
and the corresponding paths are complex.

We are ultimately interested in the
T\rightarrow\infty
limit. In this limit, the solutions to Eq. (
5.41
) at finite
k,k^{\prime}
require large
\omega_{N}
or
\omega_{P}
. As we can see in Fig.
11
, we can achieve large
\omega_{N}
with
\varepsilon\approx 0
. Such small energies correspond to starting near the top of one of the hills in the inverted potential, staying there a very long time, rolling over to the other side, staying there a long time, then rolling back. These are the famous instanton trajectories. Expanding around
\varepsilon=0
gives

\omega_{N}=-\ln\frac{-\varepsilon}{8}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left(10+3\ln\frac{-\varepsilon}{8}+\cdots\right)

(5.42)

\omega_{P}=-\pi i\left(1-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon+\frac{105}{16}\varepsilon^{2}+\cdots\right)\,.

(5.43)

Then, solving Eq. (
5.41
) we get

\varepsilon=-8e^{-i\pi\frac{k^{\prime}}{k}}e^{-\frac{T}{2k}}+\cdots\,.

(5.44)

For
k^{\prime}=0
these are the usual periodic
n=2k
instanton solutions. For nonzero
k^{\prime}
the energy
\varepsilon
has a phase rotation. This phase rotation gives
\omega_{N}
an imaginary part to cancel against
\omega_{P}=-\pi i
.

To enumerate the solutions, we first note that the pairs
(k,k^{\prime})
and
(-k,-k^{\prime})
describe the same geometric trajectory with opposite orientation (
t\to-t
), or equivalently shifting the offset by
t_{0}\to T-t_{0}
. So we can take
k>0
without loss of generality. Furthermore, taking
k^{\prime}\rightarrow k^{\prime}+2k
leaves
\varepsilon
unchanged. So an independent set of solutions is indexed by
-k<k^{\prime}\leq k
, where the solution branches
k^{\prime}
and
-k^{\prime}
are complex conjugates. We also have a choice of the turning point
x_{j}
in Eq. (
5.26
).
x_{1}
and
x_{4}
give solutions related by the
x\to-x
symmetry of
V(x)
, as do
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
, so the independent branches are labeled by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Example solutions, using the exact
\omega_{N},\omega_{P}
, are shown in Fig.
12
, for different values of
(k,k^{\prime})
and different turning points
x_{1},x_{2}
. These are all exact classical solutions at finite
T
.

Let us denote the two solution classes by
x_{(1)}(t)
and
x_{(2)}(t)
. These two branches are related by inversion: since
x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}=2
it follows from Eq. (
5.26
) that

x_{(1)}(t)\cdot x_{(2)}(t)=x_{1}\,x_{2}=\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}\,.

(5.45)

For real
\varepsilon
(i.e.
k^{\prime}=0
),
x_{(2)}(t)
crosses zero as the instanton traverses
x=0
between the two wells, so
x_{(1)}(t)=x_{1}x_{2}/x_{(2)}(t)
diverges — the trajectory escapes to
x=\pm\infty
and has infinite action. For complex
\varepsilon
(
k^{\prime}\neq 0
),
x_{(2)}(t)
acquires a nonzero imaginary part throughout its orbit, so both branches remain bounded. Since the map
x\to\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}/x
is not a symmetry of
V(x)
, the two branches are distinct saddle points with generically different actions. Both share the leading action
2kS_{I}
, and for the self-conjugate case
k^{\prime}=k
their actions are exactly equal, but for
k^{\prime}<k
they differ at subleading order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
. Therefore, the real finite-action multi-instanton solutions are all in the
x_{2}
branch and we will assume
x_{j}=x_{2}
and just write
x(t)
for these solutions from now on, unless otherwise specified.

Figure 12:
Exact instanton solutions from the Weierstrass
\wp
formula (
5.26
) with
\varepsilon
determined by the quantization condition (
5.41
), for
k=1,2
and
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
. Left:
x_{1}
branch; right:
x_{2}
branch. For real
\varepsilon
(
k^{\prime}=0
), the
x_{1}
-branch solution diverges where
x_{(2)}(t)
crosses zero, consistent with Eq. (
5.45
). For complex
\varepsilon
(
k^{\prime}\neq 0
), both branches remain bounded. Real parts in blue, imaginary parts in orange.

The action for the
(k,k^{\prime})
instantons can be computed as

S_{k,k^{\prime}}=\int_{0}^{T}dt\left[\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}^{2}-U(x)\right]=\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{k,k^{\prime}}}P_{0}\,dx-\varepsilon T\,,

(5.46)

where
\mathcal{C}_{k,k^{\prime}}
denotes the closed Euclidean trajectory winding around the elliptic curve. The momentum here is the same momentum used in Exact WKB from Eq. (
4.44
):

P_{0}(x)=\dot{x}=\sqrt{2\varepsilon-2U(x)}=\sqrt{2V(x)-2E},\qquad E=-\varepsilon.

(5.47)

We can use the same actions as derived in the Exact WKB section, rewriting Eqs. (
4.93
) and (
4.97
) in elliptic-integral form:

\displaystyle S_{P}^{0}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{3}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}}\left[E(m_{P})-\left(1-\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}\right)K(m_{P})\right],\qquad m_{P}=\frac{4\sqrt{-2\varepsilon}}{1+\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}},

(5.48)

\displaystyle S_{N}^{0}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}}\left[E(m_{N})+\left(\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}-1\right)K(m_{N})\right],\qquad m_{N}=\frac{1+8\varepsilon-\sqrt{1+8\varepsilon}}{4\varepsilon}.

(5.49)

Expanding for small
\varepsilon
(large
T
) gives

S_{N}^{0}(\varepsilon)=\frac{2}{3}-\varepsilon\!\left(\ln\frac{-\varepsilon}{8}-1\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\varepsilon^{2}\ln\varepsilon\right),\qquad S_{P}^{0}(\varepsilon)=-\pi i\,\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right).

(5.50)

The classical action for the
(k,k^{\prime})
instanton is then

S_{k,k^{\prime}}=2k\,S_{N}^{0}(\varepsilon)+2k^{\prime}\,S_{P}^{0}(\varepsilon)-\varepsilon\,T\,,

(5.51)

where
T
and
\varepsilon
are implicitly related by Eq. (
5.41
). Substituting these into the action formula and using Eq. (
5.44
) and expanding gives

\displaystyle S_{k,k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2kS_{I}-2k\,\varepsilon\!\left(\ln\frac{-\varepsilon}{8}-1\right)-2\pi ik^{\prime}\varepsilon-\varepsilon T+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\varepsilon^{2}\ln\varepsilon\right)

(5.52)

\displaystyle=2kS_{I}+2k\,\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\varepsilon^{2}\ln\varepsilon\right),

(5.53)

where
S_{I}=\frac{2}{3}
and we used
T=-2k\ln\frac{-\varepsilon}{8}-2\pi ik^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon)
. Note that there is no separate linear
k^{\prime}
term at this order: it cancels against the
-\varepsilon T
piece. There is implicit
k^{\prime}
dependence though, since

\varepsilon(T)=-8e^{-i\pi k^{\prime}/k}e^{-T/(2k)}+\cdots,

(5.54)

and the same result can alternatively be written as

S_{k,k^{\prime}}(T)=2kS_{I}-16k\,e^{-i\pi k^{\prime}/k}e^{-T/(2k)}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(T\,e^{-T/k}\right).

(5.55)

The instanton action contains the
2kS_{I}
term, confirming that only saddles with even
2k
instanton numbers contribute to the partition function, as expected from having imposed periodic boundary conditions. Note that the leading correction to the action depends on
k^{\prime}
only through the phase of an exponentially suppressed correction.

Although we have found all the bounded periodic solutions, there are also solutions that run off to infinity in
x
, such as the
k^{\prime}=0
solutions with
x_{j}=x_{1}
. It is an unusual feature of the quartic potential that it takes finite time to roll to infinity. That is

\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}(1-x^{2})^{2}+2\varepsilon}}=\frac{\omega_{N}}{2}\,.

(5.56)

For a quadratic potential this is not true – it takes an infinite amount of time to get to infinity. In any case, these solutions have infinite action so they never contribute to the path integral and we can ignore them.

In summary, the complete set of independent finite-action periodic complex saddles for the double well are given by

x(t)=x_{j}\left[1+\frac{6(x_{j}^{2}-1)}{1-3x_{j}^{2}+24\wp(t-t_{0};\omega_{P},\omega_{N})}\right]\,,

(5.57)

where
x_{j}=x_{1},x_{2}
is a turning point with
U(x_{j})=\varepsilon
,
\wp(t;\omega,\omega^{\prime})
is the Weierstrass function,
\omega_{P}
and
\omega_{N}
are the half-periods in Eq. (
5.34
), and the classical energy
\varepsilon
is quantized by

2k\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)+2k^{\prime}\omega_{P}(\varepsilon)=T\,.

(5.58)

The
k
and
k^{\prime}
are integers and independent solutions are indexed by
k>0
and
-k<k^{\prime}\leq k
(equivalently
k^{\prime}\mod 2k
), giving
2k
saddles at instanton number
n=2k
, with
k^{\prime}
and
-k^{\prime}
forming complex-conjugate pairs. These periodic saddles have even instanton number
n=2k
, and their leading action is
S_{k,k^{\prime}}=2kS_{I}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-T/(2k)})
.

5.2.3
Anti-periodic saddles

The saddles from Sec.
5.2.2
have even instanton number
n=2k
due to the periodic boundary conditions. To obtain odd-instanton saddles, we consider the twisted partition function

\widetilde{Z}(T)={\operatorname{Tr}}\!\left(\mathcal{P}\,e^{-HT/\hbar}\right)=\mathcal{N}\int_{x(0)=-x(T)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mathcal{D}}x\;e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}S[x]}\,,

(5.59)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the parity operator
x\to-x
, and the path integral runs over anti-periodic paths with
x(t+T)=-x(t)
. The saddle points of this path integral are solutions of
\ddot{x}=V^{\prime}(x)
satisfying anti-periodic boundary conditions.

To find the corresponding solutions we exploit a sign-flip property of the Weierstrass solution Eq. (
5.26
) with
x_{j}=x_{2}
: a shift by the non-perturbative half-period
\omega_{N}
flips the sign of the solution,

x(t+\omega_{N})=-x(t)\,.

(5.60)

This holds because at the modulus
t=t_{0}
the trajectory sits at
x_{2}
with
\dot{x}=0
, and after one half-period
\omega_{N}
it reaches the opposite turning point
x_{3}=-x_{2}
, again with
\dot{x}=0
. So
\tilde{x}(t)\equiv x(t+\omega_{N})
and
\hat{x}(t)\equiv-x(t)
both satisfy the equation of motion (since
V(x)
is even) with the same initial data
(-x_{2},0)
at
t=t_{0}
; by uniqueness they agree for all
t
.

Combining Eq. (
5.60
) with the full-period symmetries
x(t+2k\omega_{N})=x(t+2k^{\prime}\omega_{P})=x(t)
gives
x(t+T)=-x(t)
whenever
T=(2m+1)\omega_{N}+2k^{\prime}\omega_{P}
. The analog of Eq. (
5.41
) for anti-periodic boundary conditions is therefore

(2m+1)\,\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)+2k^{\prime}\,\omega_{P}(\varepsilon)=T\,,

(5.61)

with integers
m\geq 0
and
-m\leq k^{\prime}\leq m
(equivalently
k^{\prime}\mod(2m+1)
), giving
2m+1
saddles at instanton number
n=2m+1
, with
k^{\prime}
and
-k^{\prime}
forming complex-conjugate pairs. Their leading action at large
T
is
S_{m,k^{\prime}}^{\rm AP}=(2m+1)\,S_{I}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-T/(2m+1)})
.

The simplest case is
m=0
,
k^{\prime}=0
, for which we have the single-instanton quantization condition
\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)=T
. Rewriting the Weierstrass solution in the Jacobi elliptic form, here shifted to be defined on
t\in[0,T]
, we have

x_{\mathcal{I}}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}}\;\operatorname{sn}\!\left(\frac{t-T/2}{\sqrt{2(1+\sigma^{2})}},\,\sigma^{2}\right),

(5.62)

with
\operatorname{sn}(u,\sigma^{2})
the Jacobi elliptic sine function and
\sigma
the elliptic modulus. This modulus is related to the classical energy by

\sigma^{2}=\frac{1-\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}}{1+\sqrt{-8\varepsilon}}\,.

(5.63)

The modulus
\sigma
is the ratio of the two quarter-periods of the Jacobi elliptic functions, encoding how close the solution is to the zero-energy instanton:
\sigma\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(recovering
\operatorname{sn}(u,1)=\tanh u
), while
\sigma\to 0
corresponds to
\varepsilon\to-1/8
(the bottom of the inverted potential). We will make use of this solution in Sections
5.3.3
and
5.4.2
.

Together with Eq. (
5.26
), Eqs. (
5.41
) and (
5.61
) classify all finite-action saddle points of the double-well path integral: periodic saddles with even instanton number
n=2k
, and anti-periodic saddles with odd instanton number
n=2m+1
.

5.2.4
Approximate solutions

For large
T
, the exact solutions can be well approximated by sewing together hyperbolic tangents. This picture will prove helpful in providing a natural set of coordinates on the quasi-zero mode manifold.

The starting point is the single instanton at
T=\infty
which is a hyperbolic tangent centered at some point
t_{0}
:

x_{1}^{s}(t)=\tanh\left(\frac{t-t_{0}}{2}\right)\,.

(5.64)

This is an exact solution to the Euclidean equations of motion for any
t_{0}
, but only satisfies the boundary conditions at
T=\infty
. For finite
T
,
\tanh((t-t_{0})/2)
only satisfies
x(T/2)=-x(-T/2)
at
t_{0}=0
. Even with
t_{0}=0
, the derivatives at
t=\pm T/2
are equal rather than equal-and-opposite, so anti-periodicity (
\dot{x}(T/2)=-\dot{x}(-T/2)
) is violated and the equations of motion fail at the boundaries. In contrast, the exact
n=1
solution built from Eq. (
5.62
) with the quantization condition
\omega_{N}(\varepsilon)=T
satisfies the appropriate anti-periodic boundary conditions exactly and can be centered at any
t_{0}
. Although not exact, the
\tanh
approximate instanton and sewn-together
\tanh
approximate multi-instantons provide good approximations to the exact solutions, up to corrections that are exponentially small in
T
.

Figure 13:
Exact (Weierstrass
\wp
) and approximate (
\tanh
) 2-instanton solutions for the double well with
T=14
. The instanton is centered at
t_{1}=-T/4
, the anti-instanton at
t_{2}=T/4
, with transition point
t_{0}=0
. The action decomposes as
S=\widehat{S}(\Delta_{1})+\widehat{S}(\Delta_{2})+\widehat{S}(\Delta_{3})+\widehat{S}(\Delta_{4})
where each
\Delta
is the distance from an instanton center to the nearest boundary.

To calculate the action for the
\tanh
instanton, it is helpful to view Eq. (
5.64
) as the approximate instanton centered at
t_{0}
, with two half-instanton contributions extending a distance
T/2
from the center. On a finite interval
-\frac{T}{2}<t<\frac{T}{2}
it has action

S[x_{1}^{s}]=\widehat{S}\left(t_{0}+\frac{T}{2}\right)+\widehat{S}\left(\frac{T}{2}-t_{0}\right)\,,

(5.65)

where
\widehat{S}(\Delta)
gives the action contribution from a half-instanton extending a distance
\Delta
from its center to a boundary:

\widehat{S}(\Delta)=\int_{0}^{\Delta}dt\,\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{sech}^{4}\!\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)=\tanh\left(\frac{\Delta}{2}\right)\left[\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\cosh^{-2}\left(\frac{\Delta}{2}\right)\right]\,.

(5.66)

For large
\Delta
,

\widehat{S}(\Delta)=\frac{S_{I}}{2}\left(1-6e^{-2\Delta}+\cdots\right)

(5.67)

so that as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
S[x_{1}^{s}]\rightarrow S_{I}
. The parameter
t_{0}
can be complex; taking
t_{0}=i\pi
gives
x_{1}^{s}(t)=\coth\frac{t}{2}
, a discontinuous solution that rolls to
\pm\infty
in finite time.

For multi-instanton solutions, consider first an instanton–anti-instanton pair approximated by

x_{2}^{\text{s}}(t)=\tanh\left(\frac{t-t_{1}}{2}\right)\theta(t_{0}-t)-\tanh\left(\frac{t-t_{2}}{2}\right)\theta(t-t_{0})

(5.68)

where
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are the instanton and anti-instanton centers and
t_{0}
is the transition point. This approximate
n=2
solution is shown and compared to the exact
x_{1,0}
solution in Fig.
13
. Using the global shift symmetry to set
t_{0}=0
, periodicity forces
t_{1}=-t_{2}
, giving the action

S[x_{2}^{\text{s}}]=2\widehat{S}(t_{2})+2\widehat{S}(\dfrac{T}{2}-t_{2})\approx 2S_{I}\left(1-3e^{-2t_{2}}-3e^{-2\left(\frac{T}{2}-t_{2}\right)}\right).

(5.69)

This gives the “sewn” large-
T
action for
n=2
, where
t_{2}
plays the role of a quasi-collective-coordinate on which the action is only exponentially weakly dependent. Writing
t_{2}=t_{2}^{R}+i\phi_{2}
the saddle-point condition
\partial_{t_{2}}S[x_{2}^{\text{s}}]=0
becomes

\exp\left(-2t_{2}^{R}-2i\phi_{2}\right)-\exp\left(-T+2t_{2}^{R}+2i\phi_{2}\right)=0,

(5.70)

with solution
t_{2}^{R}=T/4
and
\phi_{2}=0
or
\phi_{2}=\pi/2
. The two distinct saddle solutions are then

x_{2}^{\text{s}}(t)=\tanh\left(\frac{t+\frac{T}{4}+i\phi_{2}}{2}\right)\theta(-t)-\tanh\left(\frac{t-\frac{T}{4}-i\phi_{2}}{2}\right)\theta(t)

(5.71)

with
\phi_{2}=0,\pi/2
, corresponding to the
(k,k^{\prime})=(1,0)
and
(1,1)
exact solutions.

Motivated by the
n=2
case, for
n=2k
we include phase shifts from the start and write

x_{2k}^{s}(t)=\sum_{p=1}^{2k}(-1)^{p+1}\tanh\left(\frac{t-t_{p}+i\phi_{p}}{2}\right)\theta(\tau_{p}<t<\tau_{p+1}).

(5.72)

Here
t_{p}
are the constituent instanton centers,
\phi_{p}
their phase offsets, and
\tau_{p}
for
p=1,\ldots,2k
are the transition points between consecutive constituents on the circle (the analog of
t_{0}
in the
n=2
case), defined cyclically with
\tau_{2k+1}\equiv\tau_{1}+T
.
The sewn action is

\displaystyle S[x_{2k}^{s}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{2k}\left[\widehat{S}(t_{p}-\tau_{p}-i\phi_{p})+\widehat{S}(\tau_{p+1}-t_{p}+i\phi_{p})\right]

(5.73)

\displaystyle\simeq 2kS_{I}-3S_{I}\sum_{p=1}^{2k}\left[e^{-2(t_{p}-\tau_{p}-i\phi_{p})}+e^{-2(\tau_{p+1}-t_{p}+i\phi_{p})}\right].

Extremizing with respect to
\tau_{p}
gives

\exp\left(-2(t_{p}-\tau_{p}-i\phi_{p})\right)=\exp\left(-2(\tau_{p}-t_{p-1}+i\phi_{p-1})\right),

(5.74)

so each nearest-neighbor separation appears twice and we have
\tau_{p}=(t_{p}+t_{p-1}-i\phi_{p}-i\phi_{p-1})/2
. Defining the complex separations directly by

\alpha_{p}\equiv(t_{p+1}-t_{p})-i(\phi_{p+1}-\phi_{p})\,,

(5.75)

and evaluating
S[x_{2k}^{s}]
on the
\tau_{p}
-extremum constraint above, we get the reduced sewn action

S_{\text{eff}}^{(s)}\simeq 2kS_{I}-6S_{I}\sum_{p=1}^{2k}e^{-\alpha_{p}}\,,

(5.76)

with periodicity constraint
\sum_{p=1}^{2k}\alpha_{p}=T
(mod
2\pi i
in the phase branch). Now extremizing all remaining quasi-collective coordinates at once using a Lagrange multiplier:

\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha_{p}}\left[S_{\text{eff}}^{(s)}+\lambda\Bigl(\sum_{q}\alpha_{q}-T\Bigr)\right]=0\;\Longrightarrow\;6S_{I}\,e^{-\alpha_{p}}+\lambda=0,\qquad p=1,\ldots,2k.

(5.77)

Because the same
\lambda
appears for every
p
, all
e^{-\alpha_{p}}
are equal. Using
\sum_{p}\alpha_{p}=T
and the phase branch condition gives

\alpha_{p}=\frac{T}{2k}-i\frac{k^{\prime}\pi}{k},\qquad k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(5.78)

Choosing the time origin so that the first instanton center is at
t=T/(4k)
,

t_{p}=\left(p-\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{T}{2k},\qquad\phi_{p}=\frac{k^{\prime}}{2k}(2p-1)\pi,\qquad\tau_{p}=(p-1)\frac{T}{2k}.

(5.79)

So the sewn construction reproduces the correct qualitative saddle structure: equally spaced constituents, constant phase increment, and a discrete branch label
k^{\prime}
(with
k^{\prime}\sim k^{\prime}+2k
and
k^{\prime}\leftrightarrow-k^{\prime}
giving conjugate branches), matching the large-
T
pattern of the exact saddles.

Finally, we can make explicit the correspondence to the two turning-point branches of the exact elliptic solutions discussed around Eq. (
5.45
). The sewn
\tanh
configurations above naturally approximate the
x_{2}
-branch saddles (the trajectories that traverse between the two wells). The second branch can be obtained without repeating the elliptic-curve analysis: indeed one checks that, for any solution
x(t)
at fixed
\varepsilon
, the inverted path
